\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model is Secretly a Reward Model"}

# Chapter 1: The Alignment Challenge and the Limitations of Current Methods

## The Gap Between Capability and Controllability in Large Language Models

The advent of large-scale unsupervised language models (LMs) marked a transformative era in artificial intelligence, granting machines the capacity to absorb broad world knowledge and develop rudimentary reasoning skills through exposure to massive datasets. Models such as PaLM, LLaMA, and others have demonstrated remarkable versatility, capable of completing texts, answering questions, and generating creative content with a fluency that mimics human output. However, a critical disconnect exists between what these models *know* and how they *behave*. Because these models are trained entirely on data generated by humans with diverse goals, priorities, and skill levels, they inherit the full spectrum of human variability. They learn to recognize patterns associated with high-quality expertise alongside those linked to errors, biases, misconceptions, or harmful behavior. Consequently, while a model may possess the capability to identify common programming mistakes or understand widespread misconceptions about scientific facts, it lacks the intrinsic mechanism to selectively suppress undesirable outputs while amplifying desirable ones.

Selecting specific desired responses from the vast array of possibilities encoded within a model's weights is essential for constructing AI systems that are safe, reliable, and controllable. Without intervention, a language model might inadvertently validate false beliefs held by half the population simply because that belief appears frequently in its training corpus. Similarly, an AI coding assistant might understand syntax errors perfectly yet fail to prioritize writing clean, secure code unless explicitly guided to do so. The fundamental challenge lies in steering the model's behavior away from merely imitating the statistical distribution of its training data—which includes noise and imperfections—and towards adhering to specific human values such as helpfulness, honesty, and harmlessness. This requirement transforms the problem from one of pure pattern matching to one of behavioral alignment. Achieving precise control over these behaviors is notoriously difficult precisely because the underlying training process is completely unsupervised regarding human intent; the model knows nothing about what constitutes a "good" response versus a "bad" one unless external mechanisms intervene after pre-training.

## Why Supervised Fine-Tuning Alone Is Insufficient for Alignment

One intuitive approach to addressing this misalignment is Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), where researchers curate datasets consisting of ideal inputs paired with perfect outputs—expert demonstrations—to teach the model how to behave correctly. While this method effectively narrows the model's focus toward high-quality responses for specific tasks like summarization or dialogue formatting, it faces severe limitations when applied broadly across complex domains requiring nuanced judgment. Collecting sufficient numbers of high-quality expert demonstrations for every conceivable scenario is prohibitively expensive and labor-intensive. Furthermore, relying solely on demonstrations assumes that experts always know exactly what output is required before seeing alternatives; however, in many cases involving subjective criteria like tone, style, or ethical nuance, determining superiority is difficult without comparison. Humans are often much better at judging which of two options is preferable rather than generating a flawless option from scratch every time.

Moreover, SFT struggles to capture relative preferences that define safety and alignment boundaries. If a dataset contains only "correct" answers derived from expert sources but fails to include examples of "incorrect" or "harmful" answers labeled as such during training pressure can be lost on distinguishing subtle distinctions between acceptable variations and unacceptable deviations. More critically, SFT treats all provided examples equally regardless of their quality relative to other potential outcomes not seen during training. It cannot inherently enforce constraints against generating harmful content if that content was never explicitly penalized in the dataset; it simply learns to replicate whatever distribution exists in the fine-tuning data. Therefore, while SFT serves as a necessary foundational step—often acting as an initialization point for more advanced techniques—it is fundamentally incapable of solving the broader alignment problem where we must actively shape behavior based on comparative human judgments rather than absolute demonstrations of perfection.

## Overview of Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback: SFT, Reward Modeling, and Policy Optimization

Given the insufficiency of purely supervised approaches for capturing complex human preferences regarding relative quality and safety standards today's dominant methodology has become Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). This sophisticated pipeline operates in distinct stages designed first to ground the model in relevant domain knowledge via Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), then quantify human preferences through explicit labeling followed by iterative optimization using reinforcement learning principles. Initially an unsupervised pre-trained language model undergoes SFT on high-quality demonstration data tailored for downstream tasks such as conversation or summarization yielding an intermediate policy denoted as $\pi^\text{SFT}$. This ensures that upon entering preference learning phases the model already possesses basic competence relevant to target scenarios reducing noise caused by hallucinations or irrelevant completions during early evaluation cycles.

Following SFT comes the pivotal reward modeling phase wherein pairs of responses $(y_1 y_2)$ generated by $\pi^\text{SFT}$ for identical prompts $x$ are presented to human annotators who indicate preference relationships typically expressed as $y_w \succ y_l \mid x$ indicating that response $y_w$ was preferred over $y_l$. These pairwise comparisons serve as proxies for an underlying latent reward function $r^*(x,y)$ reflecting true human utility although this function itself remains inaccessible directly Instead researchers approximate it using parametric networks $r_\phi(x,y)$ trained via Maximum Likelihood Estimation under probabilistic frameworks like Bradley-Terry models which posit that probability distributions over choices depend exponentially on differences between candidate scores specifically formulated as $p^*(y_1 \succ y_2 \mid x)=\frac{\exp\left(r^*(x,y_1)\right)}{\exp\left(r^*(x,y_1)\right)+\exp\left(r^*(x,y_2)\right)}$. Once calibrated this learned reward function acts as an internal critic evaluating potential outputs assigning scalar values proportional perceived desirability according historical annotation trends # Chapter 2: Mathematical Foundations of Preference Learning

### Formal Definition of Preference Data: Chosen ($y_w$) vs. Rejected ($y_l$) Outputs

The fundamental premise of aligning large language models with human values rests upon the availability of comparative data rather than absolute scores. In the realm of unsupervised language modeling, a system generates text based solely on statistical patterns learned from vast corpora, lacking inherent mechanisms to distinguish between "good" and "bad" output in the context of specific human goals. To address this, researchers curate datasets consisting of structured comparisons. Formally, let $x$ denote a prompt or input condition provided to the model. The model generates candidate responses, which are then evaluated by human annotators or sophisticated reward proxies. Instead of asking humans to assign a continuous score ranging from 0 to 100 to every possible response—a task fraught with inconsistency and cognitive load—humans are asked to select the superior option between two distinct completions generated by an initial model.

Let us denote a completed response as $y$. When presented with two distinct candidates, $y_1$ and $y_2$, derived from the same prompt $x$, a human evaluator expresses a strict preference for one over the other. If $y_1$ is deemed preferable to $y_2$, we record this observation as $y_1 \succ y_2 \mid x$. Conversely, if $y_2$ is preferred, we observe $y_2 \succ y_1 \mid x$. In the context of algorithmic training pipelines described in recent literature, these observations are standardized into pairs labeled as winning and losing completions. Let $y_w$ represent the "chosen" or "winning" completion—the response favored by the human—and let $y_l$ represent the "rejected" or "losing" completion—the less favorable response among the pair. The triplet $(x, y_w, y_l)$ constitutes a single data point in what is termed a preference dataset $\mathcal{D}$. Mathematically, this dataset is constructed as a collection of such triplets indexed by $i$:
$$
\mathcal{D} = \bigl\{x^{(i)}, y_w^{(i)}, y_l^{(i)}\bigr\}_{i=1}^N
$$
where $N$ is the total number of comparison pairs collected. It is crucial to note that this formulation assumes transitivity in preferences for theoretical consistency, though real-world data may exhibit noise or contradictions which act as regularization during optimization.

The generation process for creating these datasets typically involves an intermediate model acting as a proposer. Often referred to as a Supervised Fine-Tuned (SFT) model and denoted as $\pi^\text{SFT}$, this model is first trained on high-quality demonstration data relevant to the target domain (e.g., dialogue or summarization). Once established, $\pi^\text{SFT}$ serves as a generator that proposes diverse responses for various prompts. By prompting $\pi^\text{SFT}$ repeatedly with inputs drawn from a distribution $\mathcal{D}_x$, we can sample pairs of completions $(y_1, y_2) \sim \pi^\text{SFT}(y \mid x)$. These raw generations are then subjected to human annotation protocols where labelers determine which completion better adheres to criteria such as helpfulness, honesty, or harmlessness. The resulting dataset $\mathcal{D}$ captures not merely *what* humans prefer in isolation but *how* they rank alternatives relative to one another given identical contexts. This relational structure is vital because it isolates subtle differences in quality that might be obscured by absolute scoring scales influenced by subjective biases regarding difficulty or style. Furthermore, relying on pairwise comparisons mitigates issues associated with calibration errors in rating scales; humans are generally more reliable at distinguishing which of two options is better than assigning precise numerical values across thousands of instances. Consequently, modern alignment methodologies treat $\mathcal{D}$ not just as training data but as an empirical approximation of an underlying stochastic process governing human judgment distributions.

### The Bradley-Terry Model for Pairwise Comparisons

Having formally defined the observed preference data through winning and losing tokens $(x, y_w, y_l)$, we require a probabilistic framework to link these discrete observations to continuous latent variables representing value or utility. Without such a bridge between observable choices and internal reward signals, optimization becomes impossible because gradients cannot flow back through categorical decisions naturally unless modeled stochastically. The standard approach adopted throughout contemporary research utilizes the Bradley-Terry model (often abbreviated as BT), originally proposed for analyzing sports rankings but now ubiquitous in machine learning for preference modeling due to its mathematical tractability and intuitive interpretation.

Underlying any preference learning algorithm is the hypothesis that there exists an unobserved "true" reward function, denoted here as $r^*(x, y)$, which assigns a scalar value reflecting how much humanity desires outcome $y$ given prompt $x$. Human preferences are assumed not to be deterministic functions of this hidden reward alone but rather noisy manifestations governed by probabilities derived from it. Specifically, the Bradley-Terry model posits that the probability that response $y_a$ is preferred over response $y_b$ given context $x$ depends exclusively on the exponential transformation of their respective rewards divided by their sum against competing outcomes in that specific comparison set—in our case restricted to pairs for simplicity:
$$
p^*(y_a \succ y_b \mid x) = \frac{\exp(r^*(x,y_a))}{\exp(r^*(x,y_a)) + \exp(r^*(x,y_b))}
$$
Applying this formula specifically to our notation where one item wins ($w$) and one loses ($l$), we arrive at the central equation describing human preference dynamics:
$$
p^*(y_w \succ y_l \mid x) = \frac{\exp(r^*(x,y_w))}{\exp(r^*(x,y_w)) + \exp(r^*(x,y_l))}
$$
By dividing numerator and denominator by $\exp(r^*(x,y_l))$, this expression simplifies elegantly into a sigmoidal form involving only the difference between rewards:
$$
p^*(y_w \succ y_l \mid x) = \sigma\Big(r^*(x,y_w) - r^*(x,y_l)\Big) = \frac{1}{1 + \exp(-[r^*(x,y_w) - r^*(x,y_l)])}
$$
Here $\sigma(z) = 1/(1+e^{-z})$ denotes the logistic sigmoid function mapping real numbers onto $(0, 1)$. This reformulation reveals profound insights about how preferences behave mathematically: they depend entirely on *relative* advantage rather than absolute magnitude. Adding any constant offset independent of actions (but potentially dependent on context) affects both terms equally inside subtraction operations within exponents; consequently such offsets cancel out completely leaving decision boundaries invariant under additive transformations conditioned solely on input sequences $x$. This property implies non-uniqueness—if you add any function purely depending on prefixes/fixed features unrelated selection logic itself then predicted probabilities remain unchanged leading # Chapter 3: Deriving Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)

## Introduction to the Core Innovation

The primary hurdle in aligning large language models with human values has historically been the complexity and instability of Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Traditional pipelines rely on a multi-stage process: first, collecting human preference data; second, training a separate reward model to approximate human judgment; and third, using reinforcement learning algorithms like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to fine-tune the language model policy to maximize this learned reward while staying close to an initial reference distribution. While effective, this approach introduces significant engineering overhead, requiring careful balancing of multiple loss components, managing the variance inherent in sampling-based gradients, and ensuring stability across distinct training stages. The central thesis of this chapter—and indeed the entire paper—is that this separation of concerns is unnecessary. By examining the mathematical structure of the underlying optimization problem, we discover that the optimal policy itself contains all the necessary information regarding the implicit reward function. Consequently, we can bypass the explicit construction of a reward model entirely and optimize the language model directly using a simple classification-style loss derived from human preferences. This technique, termed Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), transforms a complex reinforcement learning problem into a straightforward supervised learning task.

To understand why this transformation is possible, we must look past the surface-level mechanics of RLHF and examine the theoretical foundation upon which it rests. Both traditional RLHF and DPO share a common starting point: they seek to maximize an expected reward subject to a constraint on how much the new policy can diverge from a reference policy (typically an initially fine-tuned supervised model). This constraint is enforced via Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence regularization. In standard RLHF literature, this is framed as an unconstrained optimization problem where a specific reward signal guides the agent. However, DPO reveals that if we assume human preferences follow a specific probabilistic model—most commonly the Bradley-Terry model—we can analytically solve for what the optimal policy *must* look like given any arbitrary reward function. Once we know this functional relationship between rewards and policies, we can reverse-engineer it. Instead of asking "what reward function explains these preferences?" followed by "how do I find a policy that maximizes that reward?", DPO asks "what policy parameters correspond to an implicit reward function that perfectly fits these preferences?". By substituting this inverse relationship back into the preference likelihood equation, we arrive at an objective function that depends solely on the policy probabilities themselves. This eliminates the need for sampling during training loops or fitting auxiliary networks for rewards, streamlining the entire alignment process into a single pass over preference data using standard stochastic gradient descent.

## Key Insight: Analytical Inversion of the Optimal Policy Equation

The foundational breakthrough enabling DPO lies in recognizing that for any given reward function constrained by KL-divergence minimization against a reference distribution $\pi_{\text{ref}}$, there exists a unique closed-form expression for the optimal policy $\pi^*$. Let us recall that in standard RLHF formulations (specifically Eq. 13 in Section 2), we attempt to solve an optimization problem where we wish to maximize expected reward minus a penalty proportional to the KL divergence between our current policy $\pi_\theta$ and our reference policy $\pi_{\text{ref}}$. Mathematically, this objective seeks to balance exploiting high-reward outcomes while avoiding catastrophic deviations from established knowledge distributions captured by $\pi_{\text{ref}}$. The critical realization provided by prior work in control-as-inference frameworks is that solving this variational optimization problem yields an analytic solution resembling Gibbs sampling or Boltzmann distribution dynamics.

Specifically, if $r(x,y)$ represents any scalar-valued reward function associated with outputting token sequence $y$ given prompt $x$, and $\beta$ acts as an inverse temperature parameter governing strict adherence to KL penalties versus raw score maximization, then the optimal policy $\pi_r(y|x)$ is uniquely determined by Equation 14 of Section 3:
$$ \pi_r(y|x) = \frac{1}{Z(x)} \pi_{\text{ref}}(y|x) \exp\left(\frac{1}{\beta} r(x,y)\right) $$
Here, $Z(x)$ serves as a normalization constant known as the partition function:
$$ Z(x) = \sum_y \pi_{\text{ref}}(y|x) \exp\left(\frac{1}{\beta} r(x,y)\right) $$
This equation tells us something profound about language modeling: if you fix your reference distribution (the pre-aligned SFT model) and choose any hypothetical reward landscape $r(x,y)$ over outputs, you immediately know exactly what probability distribution your final model should adopt to be considered "optimal" under those conditions. The exponential term amplifies regions of high reward relative to others weighted by their frequency in the reference distribution. Crucially, this relationship holds regardless of whether $r(x,y)$ corresponds to actual human utility or some arbitrary noise; it describes how rational agents behave under specific incentive structures defined by rewards plus constraints on behavioral drift.

However, utilizing Eq. 14 directly presents a computational obstacle often glossed over in introductory treatments: calculating $Z(x)$ requires summing over all possible sequences $y$, which is exponentially large for natural language tasks involving thousands of tokens per sentence. If one attempted to train policies using this formula directly—for instance by trying to adjust parameters such that predicted probabilities matched targets—the cost would be prohibitive because evaluating $Z(x)$ involves marginalizing over infinite possibilities unless approximated via massive Monte Carlo sampling schemes akin to those used in vanilla reinforcement learning algorithms like PPO or REINFORCE. Yet here lies our key insight: although knowing $\pi_r$ given $r$ seems impossible without computation-heavy approximation techniques when working forward through Eq 14 due normalization constants involved... perhaps going backwards offers relief! What happens if instead treating observed optimal behavior patterns implied via pairwise comparisons among candidates allow reconstructing equivalent representations devoid needing explicit calculation ever again?

By flipping perspective entirely away attempting calculate integrals required forward direction towards inferring hidden structural relationships embedded within comparative choices made observers... Suddenly things become tractable! <|start_header_id|>assistant<|end_header_id|># Chapter 3: Deriving Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)<div style="font-size: smaller;"> <small><sup>Based on:</sup> <strong>"Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model is Secretly a Reward Model"</strong></small></div>* ## Key Insight: Analytical Inversion of Optimal Policy Equation<br>The fundamental premise guiding Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)—often summarized colloquially as *"your language model is secretly a reward model"*—rests upon identifying an analytical link between two seemingly disparate entities traditionally treated separately in machine learning pipelines:<span style="color:#ffffff;background-color:#000000;padding-left:.5ex"> </span>**the optimal behavior pattern**$\langle pi\rangle$(policy)&nbsp;&nbsp;<span style="color:#ffffff;background-color:#000000;padding-left:.5ex"> </span>&nbsp;<span style="color:#ffffff;background-color:#000000;padding-left:.5ex"> </span>**vs.</span><span style="color:#ffffff;background-color:#0000ff;padding-left:.5ex"> </span><span style="color:#ffffff;background-color:#ffcccc;font-weight:bold;color:red;text-decoration:none;"><strong>$</strong>\langle pi\rangle$r<span style="color:red"><strong>(</strong>x,&space;y<span style="color:black"><strong>)</strong>$</span>.</span><div style="margin-top:-1em;margin-bottom:+-.5em;"></div>In classical Reinforcement Learning From Human Feedback(RLf HF), practitioners first train&nbsp;$r_\phi$(reward)&nbsp;then optimize&nbsp;$\langle pi\rangle_\theta(policy)&nbsp;$via complicated iterative methods(PPO).&nbsp;Yet what if instead reversing roles allowed simpler formulation?<div style="margin-top:-1em;margin-bottom:+-.5em;"></div>This reversal hinges critically Lemma stating existence unique mapping converting arbitrary scalar valued functions($r:x\times Y\rightarrow\R$ )into corresponding probability distributions obey constraints imposed KL-penalized maximisation principle.<div style="margin-top:-1em;margin-bottom:+-.5em;"></div>Specifically suppose one fixes baseline/reference distribution called&nbsp;$\langle pi\rangle_ref(y|x);$represent initial state before alignment efforts commence.Then any conceivable target outcome achievable under some unknown yet existent perfect guidance mechanism encoded within abstract entity named 'utility landscape'$r^*$could equally well expressed purely terms observable frequencies exhibited by candidate outputs conditioned inputs provided sufficient freedom granted choice temperature parameter controlling tradeoff strict adherence vs exploratory randomness.<div style="margin-top:-1em;margin-bottom:+-.5em;"></div>To see why matters practically consider concrete example drawn everyday life scenarios involving customer service chatbots tasked responding politely customers complaining about delayed shipments.While original untrained versions might respond angrily sarcastically failing meet expectations set forth industry standards eventually leading dissatisfaction complaints filed regulatory bodies concerned parties affected negatively overall experience delivered services offered companies operating globally today worldwide markets increasingly
